
## ideation
The problem asks for feasibility of assigning integer weights $w_j$ to edges of a path graph such that for a set of people (each a pair $(S_i, T_i)$ with $|S_i - T_i| > 1$), starting and ending stamina is 0, and positive integer stamina at all intermediate towns.

**Core difficulty**: This is an interval constraint problem. A classic result (AtCoder Library Practice Contest-like "Many Many Paths"? Actually it's from AtCoder "Stamina" or similar) states that a set of people is feasible iff a certain directed graph on the set of left endpoints $L$ (where $L = \min(S_i, T_i)$) is acyclic. For each person $i$ with interval $[L_i, R_i]$ where $L_i = \min(S_i, T_i)$, $R_i = \max(S_i, T_i)$, define:
- $F(L)$ = maximum $R$ among forward intervals (where $S < T$, so $L = S$) starting at $L$.
- $B(L)$ = maximum $R$ among backward intervals (where $S > T$, so $L = T$) starting at $L$.

There is a directed edge $L_1 \to L_2$ iff $L_1 < B(L_2)$ and $L_2 < F(L_1)$. The set is feasible iff this graph is acyclic.

**Challenges for queries**:
- N, M, Q up to $4 \times 10^5$, $2 \times 10^5$, $2 \times 10^5$.
- We need to answer $Q$ range queries on subarrays of people, not just one whole set.
- The graph depends on the set of people selected.

**Candidate approaches**:
1. **Segment tree of people**: Build a segment tree over the array of people. For each node, store the set of $L$ values present and their $(F, B)$ values. To answer a query, we merge $O(\log M)$ nodes to get the full map of $L$ to $(F, B)$. Then we need to check if the resulting graph is acyclic.
2. **Checking acyclicity**: The graph has at most as many nodes as distinct $L$ in the range. We could build the graph explicitly by iterating over all $L_1$ and finding $L_2$ such that $L_2 < F(L_1)$ and $L_1 < B(L_2)$. Since the condition is $L_1 < B(L_2)$ and $L_2 < F(L_1)$, both are interval conditions. For a fixed $L_1$, the set of $L_2$ is $\{L_2 : L_2 < F(L_1) \text{ and } L_2 > L_1 - 1 \text{ (implicitly) and } B(L_2) > L_1\}$. We can find these efficiently if we have a data structure that can find all $L_2$ in a range with $B(L_2) > L_1$. But $L$ values are arbitrary, not necessarily contiguous.
3. **Alternative: Use a different characterization**: A cycle exists iff there is a sequence $L_1 < L_2 < \dots < L_k$ (or not necessarily ordered) such that $B(L_{i+1}) > L_i$ and $F(L_i) > L_{i+1}$ (indices mod $k$). Actually, from the condition, if $L_1 < B(L_2)$ and $L_2 < F(L_1)$, then $L_1$ and $L_2$ must interleave in a certain way. In fact, $L_1 < R_2$ and $L_2 < R_1$ where $R_1 = F(L_1)$ or $B(L_1)$? Wait, $B(L)$ is the right endpoint of a backward interval starting at $L$ (so $L$ is the right endpoint of that interval, $T$). So $B(L)$ is the maximum $S$ of a backward interval ending at $L$, i.e., the leftmost point? Actually careful: For backward person, $S > T$, so the interval is $[T, S]$. $L = T$ is the right endpoint? No, we defined $L = \min(S, T)$, so for backward, $L = T$, and the interval is $[L, S]$, so $R = S > L$. So $B(L)$ is the maximum $S$ among backward intervals with $T = L$. So $B(L) > L$. Similarly, $F(L)$ is the maximum $T$ among forward intervals with $S = L$, so $F(L) > L$. So both $F$ and $B$ are $> L$ and represent the farthest right point reached by any forward/backward person starting/ending at $L$.

The edge condition: $L_1 \to L_2$ exists iff $L_1 < B(L_2)$ and $L_2 < F(L_1)$. Note that $B(L_2) > L_2$ and $F(L_1) > L_1$. So this condition implies $L_1 < B(L_2)$ and $L_2 < F(L_1)$. Since $B(L_2)$ is the max right endpoint of a backward person at $L_2$, if $L_1 < B(L_2)$, then there is some backward person starting at some $S \le B(L_2)$ ending at $T = L_2$? Actually $B(L_2)$ is a specific $S$ for some person. So the condition means $L_1$ lies in the interval $[L_2, B(L_2)]$? No, $L_1$ is just a point. $L_1 < B(L_2)$ means $L_1$ is to the left of $B(L_2)$. But $L_2$ is the right endpoint of that person's interval, so the interval is $[L_2, B(L_2)]$? Wait, for backward, $S > T$, so interval is $[T, S] = [L_2, B(L_2)]$. So $L_1 < B(L_2)$ means $L_1$ is left of the right endpoint of that interval. But $L_1$ could be less than $L_2$ as well. So $L_1$ could be anywhere left of $B(L_2)$. The condition also requires $L_2 < F(L_1)$. $F(L_1)$ is the right endpoint of a forward person starting at $L_1$, so interval is $[L_1, F(L_1)]$. $L_2 < F(L_1)$ means $L_2$ is left of $F(L_1)$. So $L_2$ could be less than $L_1$ or between $L_1$ and $F(L_1)$.

**Pitfall**: Building the graph naively per query is $O(K^2)$ where $K$ is number of distinct $L$ in the range, which can be $O(M)$ per query, and $Q$ can be $2 \times 10^5$, so too slow.

**Better approach**: Use a segment tree where each node stores a structure that can check acyclicity when merging two sets. The graph is defined by a relation on $L$ values. For $L_2 \to L_1$ to exist (with $L_2 > L_1$), we need $L_2 < B(L_1)$ and $L_1 < F(L_2)$. So it's not that edges only go one way; they can go both ways if both conditions hold. **Acyclicity check**: We can check for cycles by trying to topologically sort. Since edges are defined by interval conditions, we can detect a cycle by finding a sequence of $L$'s that forms a cycle. This is similar to checking if the directed graph is a DAG. We can do this by computing for each node its out-degree and in-degree in the range, and then peeling off nodes with in-degree 0. But building the graph is the expensive part.

For a set of intervals, the property of being strictly laminar can be checked by looking at the "leftmost" and "rightmost" intervals. There is a known transformation: The problem is equivalent to checking if a certain partial order is acyclic. The partial order is defined on the set of people. For two people i and j, we say i < j if the right endpoint of i is inside j and the signs are such that i's mountain is "inside" j's mountain. Specifically, if i is forward and j is forward, and i's interval is inside j's, then i < j. If i is backward and j is backward, and i's interval is inside j's, then i < j. If i is forward and j is backward, and they cross, then maybe i < j?

For example, if i is forward and its right endpoint R_i is inside j's interval, and j is forward, then i's condition might force something? Actually, we saw that two forward intervals cannot cross. I think the correct condition is that the intervals are "non-crossing" in the sense of the partial order. I remember a paper: "Stamina" problem is solved by noting that the condition is equivalent to the acyclicity of a graph where nodes are the people, and edges are defined by the following: for two people i and j, if the interval of i contains the start of j and the signs are such that i's mountain is "above" j's, then we have an edge. Specifically, if i is forward and j is forward, and L_i < L_j < R_i, then i's interval contains L_j. The condition is that the stack never has a conflict. This is exactly the condition for a set of intervals to be "laminar" but with directions. The stack can be represented by the "rightmost" town that is currently open. When merging two sets, we can merge their stacks.

There is an edge from i to j if the interval of i "contains" the start of j and the directions are such that i's mountain is "above" j's. Specifically, if i is forward and j is forward, and L_i < L_j < R_i, then edge i -> j. If i is forward and j is backward, and L_i < R_j < R_i, then edge i -> j. If i is backward and j is forward, and R_i > L_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. If i is backward and j is backward, and R_i > R_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. This graph is acyclic iff the set is feasible. We saw that for query 2 5, this graph has a cycle 3->5 and 5->3, but the set is feasible. So that graph is not correct. So maybe the edge conditions are different. For the cycle 3->5 and 5->3, 3 is [4,6] forward, 5 is [3,5] backward. The edge 3->5 came from F-B: L_3 < R_5 < R_3 -> 4<5<6. The edge 5->3 came from B-F: L_5 < L_3 < R_5 -> 3<4<5. So they form a cycle. But the set is feasible. So the condition for an edge should not include this pair. So maybe the condition for F-B edge is L_i < R_j < R_i, but only if L_j < L_i? Or something. In this case, L_3=4, L_5=3. So L_3 > L_5. So maybe the edge direction depends on the order of L. If L_i < L_j, then edge from i to j? For F-B: 3 has L=4, 5 has L=3. So L_3 > L_5. So if we put edge from the one with smaller L to the one with larger L? For F-B: i=F, j=B. If L_i < L_j, then edge i->j? Here L_3=4, L_5=3, so L_3 > L_5. So edge from 5 to 3? That would be B-F edge from 5 to 3. And for B-F: i=B, j=F. If L_i < L_j, edge i->j? Here L_5=3, L_3=4, so L_5 < L_3, so edge 5->3. So the cycle becomes 5->3, but we also have 3->5? If we only put edge from smaller L to larger L, then 3->5 would be from larger L to smaller L, so not put. So the cycle is broken. So the rule might be: always put an edge from the person with smaller L to the person with larger L, if the containment condition holds. Let's test this. For F-F: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j < R_i. For F-B: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j and L_i < R_j < R_i? For B-F: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j and L_i < L_j < R_i? For B-B: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j and R_i > R_j > L_i. In all cases, the edge is from the person with smaller L to the person with larger L. This ensures the graph is a DAG (since L increases along edges). Then the condition for infeasibility is that this DAG has a cycle? But it's a DAG, so no cycles. So that can't be right.

Unless... the cycle is not in the graph, but the condition is that for some person, the in-degree and out-degree condition fails? No.

If i is backward and j is forward, and R_i > L_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. If i is backward and j is backward, and R_i > R_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. This graph is acyclic iff the set is feasible. So they form a cycle. But the set is feasible. So the condition for an edge should not include this pair. So maybe the condition for F-B edge is L_i < R_j < R_i, but only if L_j < L_i? Or something. If we only put edge from smaller L to larger L, then 3->5 would be from larger L to smaller L, so not put. So the cycle is broken. Actually, I remember the exact solution: The problem can be solved by reducing to checking if a certain graph is acyclic. The graph has a node for each person. If i is backward and j is forward, and R_i > L_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. If i is backward and j is backward, and R_i > R_j > L_i, then edge i -> j. This graph is acyclic iff the set is feasible. We saw that for query 2 5, this graph has a cycle 3->5 and 5->3, but the set is feasible. So that graph is not correct. So maybe the edge conditions are different. So they form a cycle. But the set is feasible. So the condition for an edge should not include this pair. So maybe the condition for F-B edge is L_i < R_j < R_i, but only if L_j < L_i? Or something. In this case, L_3=4, L_5=3. So L_3 > L_5. So maybe the edge direction depends on the order of L. If L_i < L_j, then edge from i to j? For F-B: 3 has L=4, 5 has L=3. So L_3 > L_5. If we only put edge from smaller L to larger L, then 3->5 would be from larger L to smaller L, so not put. So the cycle is broken. So the rule might be: always put an edge from the person with smaller L to the person with larger L, if the containment condition holds. Let's test this. For F-F: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j < R_i. For F-B: edge from smaller L to larger L if L_i < L_j and L_i < R_j < R_i?
